=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums.
Any positive divisor of a natural number x that is strictly less than x is called a proper divisor of x. For example, 2 is a proper divisor of 4, while 6 is not a proper divisor of 6.
You are allowed to perform an operation any number of times on nums, where in each operation you select any one element from nums and divide it by its greatest proper divisor.
Return the minimum number of operations required to make the array non-decreasing.
If it is not possible to make the array non-decreasing using any number of operations, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [25,7]
Output: 1
Explanation:
Using a single operation, 25 gets divided by 5 and nums becomes [5, 7].

Example 2:

Input: nums = [7,7,6]
Output: -1

Example 3:

Input: nums = [1,1,1,1]
Output: 0

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
1 <= nums[i] <= 10^6

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Need maintain two DP values:

dp_reduce = min ops up to current index with current final value = spf[i].
But if nums[i] == spf[i], dp_keep and dp_reduce are same (both represent same value). We can store both equal.

Transition:
For each i, compute best_keep, best_reduce.

Let prev values:

prev_reduce_val = spf[i-1]
prev_keep_cost, prev_reduce_cost.

For current keep value K = nums[i]:
Need previous final value <= K. Among previous states with value <= K, take min cost.
prev_keep if prev_keep_val <= K; prev_reduce if prev_reduce_val <= K.

dp_keep = best_keep (no extra op).

For current reduce value R = spf[i]:
Need previous final value <= R. Take min eligible prev costs. If i==0, best=0.
dp_reduce = best + (1 if R < K else 0) because if reducing composite costs one operation; if R==K, no operation needed to have value R (it's same as keep). But wait if R==K (prime/1), dp_reduce should equal dp_keep? Since both final value same. If we compute best with threshold R=K, same as best_keep, plus0, so yes dp_reduce=dp_keep. Good. If R<K (composite), cost +1.

But note if current is composite, R is prime, R < K. Operation maps K to R in one operation. So cost1.

At the end answer = min(dp_keep, dp_reduce). If both inf -> -1.

Pseudo:
def spf_sieve(M):
    spf = list(range(M+1))
    if M>=1: spf[1]=1
    for i in range(2,int(M**0.5)+1):
        if spf[i]==i:
            step=i
            start=i*i
            for j in range(start,M+1,step):
                if spf[j]==j:
                    spf[j]=i
    return spf

In minOperations:
M=max(nums)
spf=...
INF=10**18
prev_keep_val = nums[0]
prev_keep_cost = 0
prev_red_val = spf[nums[0]]
prev_red_cost = 0 if prev_red_val == prev_keep_val else 1
# But if nums[0]=1, spf[1]=1, cost0.

for i in range(1,n):
    cur = nums[i]
    cur_red = spf[cur]
    # compute best for keep
    best_keep = INF
    if prev_keep_val <= cur:
        best_keep = min(best_keep, prev_keep_cost)
    if prev_red_val <= cur:
        best_keep = min(best_keep, prev_red_cost)
    cur_keep_cost = best_keep  # if INF then inf

    best_red = INF
    if prev_keep_val <= cur_red:
        best_red = min(best_red, prev_keep_cost)
    if prev_red_val <= cur_red:
        best_red = min(best_red, prev_red_cost)
    cur_red_cost = best_red + (1 if cur_red < cur else 0)
    # But if best_red INF, adding yields INF+1; need handle. Use if best_red < INF.
    if best_red >= INF:
        cur_red_cost = INF
    else:
        cur_red_cost = best_red + (1 if cur_red < cur else 0)

    prev_keep_val, prev_keep_cost = cur, cur_keep_cost
    prev_red_val, prev_red_cost = cur_red, cur_red_cost

ans = min(prev_keep_cost, prev_red_cost)
return -1 if ans >= INF else ans

But let's not be complacent. Let's derive operation more formally:
For x > 1, let p be smallest prime divisor. The divisors of x less than x include x/p (since p>1). Is x/p the greatest proper divisor? Need prove. Any proper divisor d < x. Let q = x/d. Since d proper, q >1 integer. If d > x/p, then q = x/d < p. But q is an integer divisor of x greater than 1, so q has a prime factor <= q < p, contradicting p smallest. Thus no proper divisor > x/p. So greatest proper divisor = x/p. Then x / (x/p) = p. Thus operation maps x to smallest prime factor. Good.

Now, if x is prime, p=x, greatest proper divisor=1, operation maps x to x. Our formula p=x also works if we define spf(x)=x. For x=1, spf undefined but we set 1.

Thus reachable values:
- x=1: {1}
- x prime: {x}
- x composite: {x, p} where p=spf(x) (prime). Actually can we perform operation twice? First x->p. Second p->p (no change). So yes.

Now DP correctness:
Let `dp0[i]` = min ops for prefix up to i with i-th final value = nums[i].
Let `dp1[i]` = min ops for prefix up to i with i-th final value = spf(nums[i]) (if composite; if prime same).
Transition as above. Need ensure `dp1` for prime with same value as `dp0` but maybe if we set cost 0. If we set cost 1 for prime, then `dp1` would be dp0+1, never better but could affect answer? It would not affect min if dp0 exists. But transitions might use dp1 as previous if value <= threshold, but dp0 with same value and lower cost is also eligible, so dp1 irrelevant. So even if cost 1 for prime, answer still correct? For current prime, dp1 = best_red+1; dp0=best_keep with same threshold. Since best_red=best_keep, dp1=dp0+1, so min unaffected. But if we use dp1 as previous for future, dp0 same value lower cost eligible, so no harm. But better set cost0 for no change. For 1, if cost1 would be wrong? Operation impossible but if set cost1, dp1=dp0+1; dp0 same value lower cost eligible, so answer still correct? For n=1, min dp0=0; okay. For transitions, dp0 lower. So harmless but not elegant. We'll set cost0 if spf==nums.

Potential issue with spf sieve: `spf = list(range(M+1))` for M=1e6 okay. For M=0? constraints M>=1. For M=1, spf length2? Actually range(2) [0,1]. spf[1]=1. Loop range(2, int(sqrt(1))+1) = range(2,2) none. Good.
If M=1, max=1, spf[1]=1. Good.

Sieve code:
def build_spf(n):
    spf = list(range(n + 1))
    if n >= 1:
        spf[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if spf[i] == i:
            for j in range(i * i, n + 1, i):
                if spf[j] == j:
                    spf[j] = i
    return spf
For n=0? not needed. For n=1, spf[0]=0, spf[1]=1. If nums contains 1, spf[1]=1. Good.

Does this sieve set spf for composite numbers that are product of two primes both > sqrt(n)? Impossible for composite <= n: at least one factor <= sqrt(n). So okay. For prime p > sqrt(n), spf[p]=p from list(range). For composite like 999983*? >1e6 no. Good.

Let's test spf for 49: list range spf[49]=49; i=7, spf[7]=7, j=49, spf[49]==49 set to7. Good.
For 25: i=5, j=25 set5. For 27: i=3, j=9,18,27; spf[27]=3. Good.
For 12: i=2, j=4,6,8,10,12; spf[12]=2. Good.
For 6: i=2, j=4,6; spf[6]=2. Good.
For 15: i=3, j=9,12,15; spf[15]=3. Good.
For 77: i=7, j=49,56,63,70,77; spf[77]=7. Good.
For 91=7*13: i=7, j=49...91 set7. Good.
For 121: i=11, j=121 set11. Good.

Time O(M log log M) plus O(n). Fine.

ans=0
next_val = nums[-1]
for i in range(n-2, -1, -1):
    x = nums[i]
    if x <= next_val:
        next_val = x
    else:
        p = spf[x]
        if p <= next_val:
            ans += 1
            next_val = p
        else:
            return -1
return ans

We process from right to left. Let `nxt` be the final value chosen for index i+1 in an optimal solution? Actually algorithm chooses for each suffix a value. Consider any feasible solution for entire array. Look at the last index n-1: It has no upper bound; choosing its original value is always feasible and minimizes operations and maximizes its value. So there is an optimal solution with f_{n-1}=nums[n-1]. Greedy does that.

Inductive step: Suppose for some i+1, greedy has fixed f_{i+1}=U, and there exists an optimal solution for prefix 0..i+1 with f_{i+1}=U? Actually need ensure no alternative lower U could reduce total ops? Lowering U (by reducing i+1) costs more and tightens constraint for i, so cannot reduce ops for prefix. But maybe lowering i+1 could allow i to be kept? Wait constraint f_i <= f_{i+1}. If f_{i+1} is lower, then f_i must be <= lower, which is stricter, so if f_i was <= higher, it may not be <= lower. Thus lowering U cannot allow i to be kept if it wasn't already; it can only force more reductions. It also adds cost. Therefore the highest feasible U is optimal. This is the key. For current i, given U, if nums[i] <= U, keeping is feasible, cost 0, and gives largest possible f_i (nums[i]) which is the most permissive upper bound for i-1. Any solution that reduces i would have higher cost and lower f_i, making i-1 constraint stricter; can replace by keeping without breaking i<=i+1 and without increasing cost. Thus greedy keep. If nums[i] > U, keeping is infeasible. The only way to satisfy f_i <= U is to reduce to spf[i] (if composite) and if spf[i] <= U. If spf[i] > U, impossible. If spf[i] <= U, reduction is forced; cost +1 and f_i=spf[i], the largest feasible (only) value. Thus greedy is optimal.

This proof is solid. Greedy from right is even more efficient and less code. But DP is also fine. Which to final? Greedy code is shorter and less prone to DP transition bugs. Need decide. Thus greedy code:
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        max_val = max(nums)
        spf = list(range(max_val + 1))
        if max_val >= 1:
            spf[1] = 1
        for i in range(2, int(max_val ** 0.5) + 1):
            if spf[i] == i:
                for j in range(i*i, max_val + 1, i):
                    if spf[j] == j:
                        spf[j] = i
        ans = 0
        nxt = nums[-1]
        for i in range(len(nums)-2, -1, -1):
            x = nums[i]
            if x <= nxt:
                nxt = x
            else:
                p = spf[x]
                if p <= nxt:
                    ans += 1
                    nxt = p
                else:
                    return -1
        return ans

Now, let's think about the problem statement "Any positive divisor of a natural number x that is strictly less than x is called a proper divisor of x. For example, 2 is a proper divisor of 4, while 6 is not a proper divisor of 6." They say "You are allowed to perform an operation any number of times on nums, where in each operation you select any one element from nums and divide it by its greatest proper divisor." For 4, greatest proper divisor is2, 4/2=2. For 6, greatest proper divisor is3, 6/3=2. So yes.

Now, maybe if x=1, greatest proper divisor doesn't exist. The operation says "select any one element and divide it by its greatest proper divisor"; if element is1, no greatest proper divisor, so operation cannot be applied. Our model doesn't apply operation to1. Good.

Could implement linear sieve for memory and speed? Linear sieve O(M) and simple:
spf = [0]*(M+1)
primes = []
for i in range(2, M+1):
    if spf[i] == 0:
        spf[i] = i
        primes.append(i)
    for p in primes:
        v = p * i
        if v > M or p > spf[i]:
            break
        spf[v] = p
Need set spf[1]=1. This is standard. Complexity O(M). For M=1e6, fine. But nested loop with break is okay. Need ensure `p > spf[i]` condition correct. Standard linear sieve:
for i in range(2,n+1):
    if spf[i]==0:
        spf[i]=i; primes.append(i)
    for p in primes:
        if p > spf[i] or i*p > n: break
        spf[i*p]=p
This ensures each composite assigned once. For i=2, spf[2]=2, primes [2], p=2, p>spf? 2>2 false, v=4 assign2. For i=3, spf[3]=3, p=2, 2>3 false assign6=2; p=3, 3>3 false assign9=3. We can set spf[1]=1 and start loop at2. For primes, spf[i]=i. For composites, assigned before reaching i. Good.

def build_spf(n):
    spf = [0] * (n + 1)
    if n >= 1:
        spf[1] = 1
    primes = []
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        if spf[i] == 0:
            spf[i] = i
            primes.append(i)
        for p in primes:
            v = p * i
            if v > n or p > spf[i]:
                break
            spf[v] = p
    return spf

Let's ensure code uses `List` imported. Final:
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        max_val = max(nums)
        spf = [0] * (max_val + 1)
        if max_val >= 1:
            spf[1] = 1

        for i in range(2, max_val + 1):
            if spf[i] == 0:
                spf[i] = i
                if i * i <= max_val:
                    for j in range(i * i, max_val + 1, i):
                        if spf[j] == 0:
                            spf[j] = i

        ans = 0
        nxt = nums[-1]

        for i in range(len(nums) - 2, -1, -1):
            x = nums[i]
            if x <= nxt:
                nxt = x
            else:
                p = spf[x]
                if p <= nxt:
                    ans += 1
                    nxt = p
                else:
                    return -1

        return ans
```

Let's perhaps think about an alternative DP if greedy somehow fails due to "minimum operations" and "non-decreasing" maybe if reducing current to spf (lower) could allow previous to be kept? Wait constraint previous <= current. If current lower, previous must be <= lower. That is stricter. So no. The only way lowering current helps is if current > next; then it satisfies current <= next. It doesn't help left. Thus right-to-left greedy is optimal. This is a strong invariant.

Let's formalize in mind:
For each i, once f_{i+1} is fixed to the largest possible value in some optimal solution, choose f_i as the largest possible value not exceeding f_{i+1}. If no such value, impossible. This greedy minimizes operations for prefix because:
- It minimizes cost at i (largest value = fewest operations).
- It maximizes f_i, which relaxes the constraint for i-1.
Thus by induction.

Now, let's think if "largest possible value not exceeding f_{i+1}" for current is:
- if nums[i] <= f_{i+1}: nums[i]
- else if spf[nums[i]] <= f_{i+1}: spf[nums[i]]
- else impossible.
Because reachable values are {nums[i], spf[nums[i]]} (with duplicates). Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sieve" if max_val=1000000 and spf[999931]? Good.

Now, let's consider if
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
with code.</think>```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        max_val = max(nums)

        # spf[x] = smallest prime factor of x
        # For prime x, spf[x] = x; for 1, spf[1] = 1.
        spf = [0] * (max_val + 1)
        if max_val >= 1:
            spf[1] = 1

        for i in range(2, max_val + 1):
            if spf[i] == 0:
                spf[i] = i
                if i * i <= max_val:
                    for j in range(i * i, max_val + 1, i):
                        if spf[j] == 0:
                            spf[j] = i

        ans = 0
        nxt = nums[-1]

        # Process from right to left.
        # For each element, the only useful operation is changing it to its spf.
        for i in range(len(nums) - 2, -1, -1):
            x = nums[i]
            if x <= nxt:
                nxt = x
            else:
                p = spf[x]
                if p <= nxt:
                    ans += 1
                    nxt = p
                else:
                    return -1

        return ans
```
